Description\n\n\n\nTracks onsite interactions and events for analytics, enabling session identification, feature usage measurement, and product optimization through PostHog across visits.\n\n\n- Cookie\n\n\\_ppc\\_customerStatus\n\n- Duration\n\nsession\n\n- Description\n\n\n\nDifferentiates new versus existing customers for PPC tracking, enabling targeted messaging, accurate conversion attribution, audience segmentation, and optimized advertising spend.\n\n\n- Cookie\n\nirtps\n\n- Duration\n\n1 day\n\n- Description\n\n\n\nDescription is currently not available.\n\n\n- Cookie\n\nai\\_user\n\n- Duration\n\n1 year\n\n- Description\n\nMicrosoft Azure sets this cookie as a unique user identifier cookie, enabling counting of the number of users accessing the application over time.\n\n\nUncategorized\n\nOther uncategorized cookies are those that are being analyzed and have not been classified into a category as yet.\n\nNo cookies to display.\n\nPurposes & Features\n\nPurposes (11)\n\nStore and/or access information on a device\n\nConsent\n\nCookies, device or similar online identifiers (e.g. login-based identifiers, randomly assigned identifiers, network based identifiers) together with other information (e.g. browser type and information, language, screen size, supported technologies etc.) can be stored or read on your device to recognise it each time it connects to an app or to a website, for one or several of the purposes presented here.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- Most purposes explained in this notice rely on the storage or accessing of information from your device when you use an app or visit a website. For example, a vendor or publisher might need to store a cookie on your device during your first visit on a website, to be able to recognise your device during your next visits (by accessing this cookie each time).\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 860\n\nUse limited data to select advertising\n\nLegitimate Interest\n\nConsent\n\nAdvertising presented to you on this service can be based on limited data, such as the website or app you are using, your non-precise location, your device type or which content you are (or have been) interacting with (for example, to limit the number of times an ad is presented to you).\n\nIllustrations\n\n- A car manufacturer wants to promote its electric vehicles to environmentally conscious users living in the city after office hours. The advertising is presented on a page with related content (such as an article on climate change actions) after 6:30 p.m. to users whose non-precise location suggests that they are in an urban zone.\n- A large producer of watercolour paints wants to carry out an online advertising campaign for its latest watercolour range, diversifying its audience to reach as many amateur and professional artists as possible and avoiding showing the ad next to mismatched content (for instance, articles about how to paint your house). The number of times that the ad has been presented to you is detected and limited, to avoid presenting it too often.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent or relying on legitimate interest: 803\n\nCreate profiles for personalised advertising\n\nConsent\n\nInformation about your activity on this service (such as forms you submit, content you look at) can be stored and combined with other information about you (for example, information from your previous activity on this service and other websites or apps) or similar users. This is then used to build or improve a profile about you (that might include possible interests and personal aspects). Your profile can be used (also later) to present advertising that appears more relevant based on your possible interests by this and other entities.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- If you read several articles about the best bike accessories to buy, this information could be used to create a profile about your interest in bike accessories. Such a profile may be used or improved later on, on the same or a different website or app to present you with advertising for a particular bike accessory brand. If you also look at a configurator for a vehicle on a luxury car manufacturer website, this information could be combined with your interest in bikes to refine your profile and make an assumption that you are interested in luxury cycling gear.\n- An apparel company wishes to promote its new line of high-end baby clothes. It gets in touch with an agency that has a network of clients with high income customers (such as high-end supermarkets) and asks the agency to create profiles of young parents or couples who can be assumed to be wealthy and to have a new child, so that these can later be used to present advertising within partner apps based on those profiles.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 645\n\nUse profiles to select personalised advertising\n\nConsent\n\nAdvertising presented to you on this service can be based on your advertising profiles, which can reflect your activity on this service or other websites or apps (like the forms you submit, content you look at), possible interests and personal aspects.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- An online retailer wants to advertise a limited sale on running shoes. It wants to target advertising to users who previously looked at running shoes on its mobile app. Tracking technologies might be used to recognise that you have previously used the mobile app to consult running shoes, in order to present you with the corresponding advertisement on the app.\n- A profile created for personalised advertising in relation to a person having searched for bike accessories on a website can be used to present the relevant advertisement for bike accessories on a mobile app of another organisation.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 648\n\nCreate profiles to personalise content\n\nConsent\n\nInformation about your activity on this service (for instance, forms you submit, non-advertising content you look at) can be stored and combined with other information about you (such as your previous activity on this service or other websites or apps) or similar users. This is then used to build or improve a profile about you (which might for example include possible interests and personal aspects). Your profile can be used (also later) to present content that appears more relevant based on your possible interests, such as by adapting the order in which content is shown to you, so that it is even easier for you to find content that matches your interests.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- You read several articles on how to build a treehouse on a social media platform. This information might be added to a profile to mark your interest in content related to outdoors as well as do-it-yourself guides (with the objective of allowing the personalisation of content, so that for example you are presented with more blog posts and articles on treehouses and wood cabins in the future).\n- You have viewed three videos on space exploration across different TV apps. An unrelated news platform with which you have had no contact builds a profile based on that viewing behaviour, marking space exploration as a topic of possible interest for other videos.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 266\n\nUse profiles to select personalised content\n\nConsent\n\nContent presented to you on this service can be based on your content personalisation profiles, which can reflect your activity on this or other services (for instance, the forms you submit, content you look at), possible interests and personal aspects. This can for example be used to adapt the order in which content is shown to you, so that it is even easier for you to find (non-advertising) content that matches your interests.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- You read articles on vegetarian food on a social media platform and then use the cooking app of an unrelated company. The profile built about you on the social media platform will be used to present you vegetarian recipes on the welcome screen of the cooking app.\n- You have viewed three videos about rowing across different websites. An unrelated video sharing platform will recommend five other videos on rowing that may be of interest to you when you use your TV app, based on a profile built about you when you visited those different websites to watch online videos.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 239\n\nMeasure advertising performance\n\nLegitimate Interest\n\nConsent\n\nInformation regarding which advertising is presented to you and how you interact with it can be used to determine how well an advert has worked for you or other users and whether the goals of the advertising were reached. For instance, whether you saw an ad, whether you clicked on it, whether it led you to buy a product or visit a website, etc. This is very helpful to understand the relevance of advertising campaigns.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- You have clicked on an advertisement about a “black Friday” discount by an online shop on the website of a publisher and purchased a product. Your click will be linked to this purchase. Your interaction and that of other users will be measured to know how many clicks on the ad led to a purchase.\n- You are one of very few to have clicked on an advertisement about an “international appreciation day” discount by an online gift shop within the app of a publisher. The publisher wants to have reports to understand how often a specific ad placement within the app, and notably the “international appreciation day” ad, has been viewed or clicked by you and other users, in order to help the publisher and its partners (such as agencies) optimise ad placements.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent or relying on legitimate interest: 929\n\nMeasure content performance\n\nLegitimate Interest\n\nConsent\n\nInformation regarding which content is presented to you and how you interact with it can be used to determine whether the (non-advertising) content e.g. reached its intended audience and matched your interests. For instance, whether you read an article, watch a video, listen to a podcast or look at a product description, how long you spent on this service and the web pages you visit etc. This is very helpful to understand the relevance of (non-advertising) content that is shown to you.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- You have read a blog post about hiking on a mobile app of a publisher and followed a link to a recommended and related post. Your interactions will be recorded as showing that the initial hiking post was useful to you and that it was successful in interesting you in the related post. This will be measured to know whether to produce more posts on hiking in the future and where to place them on the home screen of the mobile app.\n- You were presented a video on fashion trends, but you and several other users stopped watching after 30 seconds. This information is then used to evaluate the right length of future videos on fashion trends.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent or relying on legitimate interest: 406\n\nUnderstand audiences through statistics or combinations of data from different sources\n\nLegitimate Interest\n\nConsent\n\nReports can be generated based on the combination of data sets (like user profiles, statistics, market research, analytics data) regarding your interactions and those of other users with advertising or (non-advertising) content to identify common characteristics (for instance, to determine which target audiences are more receptive to an ad campaign or to certain contents).\n\nIllustrations\n\n- The owner of an online bookstore wants commercial reporting showing the proportion of visitors who consulted and left its site without buying, or consulted and bought the last celebrity autobiography of the month, as well as the average age and the male/female distribution of each category. Data relating to your navigation on its site and to your personal characteristics is then used and combined with other such data to produce these statistics.\n- An advertiser wants to better understand the type of audience interacting with its adverts. It calls upon a research institute to compare the characteristics of users who interacted with the ad with typical attributes of users of similar platforms, across different devices. This comparison reveals to the advertiser that its ad audience is mainly accessing the adverts through mobile devices and is likely in the 45-60 age range.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent or relying on legitimate interest: 586\n\nDevelop and improve services\n\nLegitimate Interest\n\nConsent\n\nInformation about your activity on this service, such as your interaction with ads or content, can be very helpful to improve products and services and to build new products and services based on user interactions, the type of audience, etc. This specific purpose does not include the development or improvement of user profiles and identifiers.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- A technology platform working with a social media provider notices a growth in mobile app users, and sees based on their profiles that many of them are connecting through mobile connections. It uses a new technology to deliver ads that are formatted for mobile devices and that are low-bandwidth, to improve their performance.\n- An advertiser is looking for a way to display ads on a new type of consumer device. It collects information regarding the way users interact with this new kind of device to determine whether it can build a new mechanism for displaying advertising on this type of device.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent or relying on legitimate interest: 690\n\nUse limited data to select content\n\nLegitimate Interest\n\nConsent\n\nContent presented to you on this service can be based on limited data, such as the website or app you are using, your non-precise location, your device type, or which content you are (or have been) interacting with (for example, to limit the number of times a video or an article is presented to you).\n\nIllustrations\n\n- A travel magazine has published an article on its website about the new online courses proposed by a language school, to improve travelling experiences abroad. The school’s blog posts are inserted directly at the bottom of the page, and selected on the basis of your non-precise location (for instance, blog posts explaining the course curriculum for different languages than the language of the country you are situated in).\n- A sports news mobile app has started a new section of articles covering the most recent football games. Each article includes videos hosted by a separate streaming platform showcasing the highlights of each match. If you fast-forward a video, this information may be used to select a shorter video to play next.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent or relying on legitimate interest: 181\n\nSpecial Purposes (3)\n\nEnsure security, prevent and detect fraud, and fix errors\n\nYour data can be used to monitor for and prevent unusual and possibly fraudulent activity (for example, regarding advertising, ad clicks by bots), and ensure systems and processes work properly and securely. It can also be used to correct any problems you, the publisher or the advertiser may encounter in the delivery of content and ads and in your interaction with them.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- An advertising intermediary delivers ads from various advertisers to its network of partnering websites. It notices a large increase in clicks on ads relating to one advertiser, and uses data regarding the source of the clicks to determine that 80% of the clicks come from bots rather than humans.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 663\n\nDeliver and present advertising and content\n\nCertain information (like an IP address or device capabilities) is used to ensure the technical compatibility of the content or advertising, and to facilitate the transmission of the content or ad to your device.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- Clicking on a link in an article might normally send you to another page or part of the article. To achieve this, 1°) your browser sends a request to a server linked to the website, 2°) the server answers back (“here is the article you asked for”), using technical information automatically included in the request sent by your device, to properly display the information / images that are part of the article you asked for. Technically, such exchange of information is necessary to deliver the content that appears on your screen.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 648\n\nSave and communicate privacy choices\n\nThe choices you make regarding the purposes and entities listed in this notice are saved and made available to those entities in the form of digital signals (such as a string of characters). This is necessary in order to enable both this service and those entities to respect such choices.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- When you visit a website and are offered a choice between consenting to the use of profiles for personalised advertising or not consenting, the choice you make is saved and made available to advertising providers, so that advertising presented to you respects that choice.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 527\n\nFeatures (3)\n\nThese means of processing can be used solely in pursuit of one or several purposes for which you are given a choice in this notice.\n\nMatch and combine data from other data sources\n\nInformation about your activity on this service may be matched and combined with other information relating to you and originating from various sources (for instance your activity on a separate online service, your use of a loyalty card in-store, or your answers to a survey), in support of the purposes explained in this notice.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- You used your loyalty card when purchasing sunglasses at a local boutique. If you later browse content relating to summer travel, this information may be combined with your previous purchase recorded by the boutique’s loyalty program to present you with ads for swimsuits or other beach accessories.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 467\n\nLink different devices\n\nIn support of the purposes explained in this notice, your device might be considered as likely linked to other devices that belong to you or your household (for instance because you are logged in to the same service on both your phone and your computer, or because you may use the same Internet connection on both devices).\n\nIllustrations\n\n- You visited an online retailer website from your computer to look at mystery books and you consented to your data being used to create profiles for personalised advertising. If you later browse on your phone using the same Wifi, a vendor may infer you have been using both devices and you may be presented with an ad about the same mystery books in the mobile app you are using, but only if you have consented to receiving personalised advertising on this app.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 379\n\nIdentify devices based on information transmitted automatically\n\nYour device might be distinguished from other devices based on information it automatically sends when accessing the Internet (for instance, the IP address of your Internet connection or the type of browser you are using) in support of the purposes exposed in this notice.\n\nIllustrations\n\n- When you see an ad in your weather forecast app, some technical details that your device always transmits when accessing the Internet (such as the IP address of your Wifi and the version of your device’s operating system) can be used for instance to limit the number of times you see the same ad in other apps.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 602\n\nSpecial Features (2)\n\nUse precise geolocation data\n\nConsent\n\nWith your acceptance, your precise location (within a radius of less than 500 metres) may be used in support of the purposes explained in this notice.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 290\n\nIdentify devices based on information actively requested\n\nConsent\n\nWith your acceptance, certain characteristics specific to your device might be requested and used to distinguish it from other devices (such as the installed fonts or plugins, the resolution of your screen) in support of the purposes explained in this notice.\n\nNumber of Vendors seeking consent: 169\n\nVendors\n\nThird party vendors (1037)\n\nNoSave My PreferencesYes\n\n# [Perth](https://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/australia/perth.html) Welcome to Perth: Preparing For Your Trip\n\n[Back to Perth](https://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/australia/perth.html)\n\nShare\n\n# Welcome To Perth: Preparing For Your Trip\n\n### How to Get Around Perth, Western Australia’s Largest City\n\nAre you planning a trip to Perth? The capital of Western Australia, Perth offers an exciting mix of cosmopolitan pleasures and verdant scenery.\n\nA gateway to the famous Margaret River region of lush vineyards, Perth is also near historic Fremantle and Rottnest island, known for rich wildlife and pristine beaches.\n\nBookmark [Pan Pacific Perth](https://www.panpacific.com/en/hotels-and-resorts/pp-perth.html)'s handy guide to getting around Perth and get the most out of your visit.\n\n### Travelling from Perth Airport to the City\n\n![ perth airport taxis](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/taxi-perth-airport.jpg)\n\n![ perth airport taxis](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/taxi-perth-airport.jpg)\n\n_Image: Shutterstock_\n\nPublic transportation out of [Perth airport](https://www.perthairport.com.au/) can be tricky because it has no rail line to the city. Depending on how much time you have to get downtown, choose from a private charter vehicle, taxi, or bus.\n\nPrivate charter vehicles may be ideal if you are on a tight schedule or with a group. Drivers will meet and escort you to your vehicle. Vehicle services include [Exceed Chauffeur Services](https://www.tripadvisor.com.sg/Attraction_Review-g255103-d7697553-Reviews-Exceed_Chauffeur_Services-Perth_Greater_Perth_Western_Australia.html) (+61 410 669 525) and [GranTourismo Range Rover](http://www.grantourismo.com.au/) (+61 419 998 887).\n\nPrefer to hail a cab? Taxi stands are located outside each terminal. Or dial +61 131 008 for Black and White Cabs that serve Perth, or +61 13 13 30 for Swan Taxis. Uber is also available in Perth.\n\nConsider [airport bus services to Elizabeth Quay Bus Station downtown](http://www.perthairport.com.au/to-and-from-the-airport/transport-options/public-transport) if you are not in a hurry. Transperth bus 380 travels from terminals 1 and 2, and bus 40 runs from terminals 3 and 4 to Elizabeth Quay Bus Station.\n\n### Getting Around Perth City: Bus, Car, Train, and Taxi Options\n\nPlease accept cookies to access this content.\n\nClean and safe, Perth’s public transit system is formed by an extensive network of buses, trains, and ferries.\n\nGovernment-operated under Transperth, Perth’s [railway system](http://www.transperth.wa.gov.au/timetables/results?train=) and buses allow you to travel with a reusable SmartRider card that cuts 15%-25% off regular fares. SmartRider cards are available at Transperth Information Centres and retail outlets across town.\n\nIf you are staying downtown, you can also hop on and off the free [Central Area Transit (CAT)](http://www.transperth.wa.gov.au/timetables/cat-timetables) buses that run daily in the city centre. CAT bus stops also have real-time route information that keeps you informed about bus arrival times.\n\nSimilarly, travel for free on any bus within the city’s free transit zone (FTZ). When travelling outside the Perth FTZ, however, do buy a ticket on board or pay with a SmartRider card.\n\nTaxi stands are also located conveniently around the city. If you prefer to travel on water, you could catch the scenic [Transperth ferry service](http://www.transperth.wa.gov.au/timetables/results?Ferry=) between Elizabeth Quay Jetty and Mends St Jetty near the Perth Zoo. This ferry route runs long hours daily.\n\n### Getting To Know Perth’s Weather\n\n![perth cycling in good weather](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/swan-river-perth.jpg)\n\n![perth cycling in good weather](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/swan-river-perth.jpg)\n\n_Image: Pixabay_\n\nUnlike cities in Australia’s southern tip, Perth weather is generally mild and comfortable. Dry and balmy summer falls between December to February as Perth is in the southern hemisphere. Temperatures range from 18°C to 30°C (64°F to 86°F) then, with breezy afternoons blowing the heat away.\n\nAutumn in Perth from March to May is marked by warm, sunny days and comfortably cool nights. Temperatures fall between 14°C to 26°C (57°F to 79°F) with evening temperatures rarely dropping below 10°C (50°F). Occasional showers and humidity can crop up in autumn, however, so bring your favourite umbrella.\n\nRain and thunderstorms are common in winter, from June to August. Temperatures may fluctuate more widely during this season, so do dress in layers including a waterproof outer layer if possible. It can get quite chilly at night with temperatures running from 8°C to 19°C (47°F to 66°F) in winter.\n\nSpring arrives in Perth from September to November with sunny days and little rain. Daily temperatures range from 12°C to 23°C (53°F to 73°F). Wildflowers blossom in Perth’s parks and gardens at this time, so if you suffer from hay fever, pop your medication in your luggage!\n\n### Where To Eat and Drink in Perth\n\n![ perth cbd restaurant](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/montereys-restaurant-perth.jpg)\n\n![ perth cbd restaurant](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/montereys-restaurant-perth.jpg)\n\n_Image: Pan Pacific Perth_\n\nPerfect for a meal with a colleague or client, Montereys Restaurant at Pan Pacific Perth serves Australian and international cuisines at breakfast, lunch, and dinner. Our chefs’ offerings include fresh salads, homemade soups, Asian-inspired hot dishes, daily selections from a live cooking station, and a dessert bar with sweet treats. Notable grill items include rib eye on the bone, beef fillet, wagyu rump, and lamb rack.\n\nIf you are feeling hungry from all that travelling, check out the CBD for dining options. You’ll find award-winning restaurants opened by top chefs such as Jamie Oliver and Matt Stone.\n\nPopular restaurants near our hotel include Friends Restaurant with modern Australian fine dining. Located in Fortescue Centre, it’s top-ranked in East Perth by TripAdvisor users and offers a stunning Swan River view. Trained in Lyon, France, chef Frederic Verschoore has worked in multiple Michelin-starred restaurants.\n\nModern southern Mediterranean cuisine can be found at The Partisan in East Perth. Dinner specials include rabbit and rosemary terrine, duck liver parfait, roasted half chermoula chicken, and pomegranate braised lamb. And for northern and southern Indian cuisine, hit up Balti, open 365 days a year. Balti also welcomes functions and parties of up to 110 people.\n\n### Where to Shop in Perth\n\n![perth city shopping street](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/perth-city-hay-street-mall.jpg)\n\n![perth city shopping street](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/perth-city-hay-street-mall.jpg)\n\nShopping for souvenirs in Perth? Check out the city’s Murray and Hay Street Malls, West End and Wesley Quarter, and Watertown Brand Outlet Centre.\n\nFor a trip out of the city, consider spending your Aussie dollars in suburban shopping locations such as Subiaco, Claremont, Cottesloe, and Fremantle. Scoot down from Perth via the Fremantle train that Experience Perth recommends. Peruse the Visit Perth City website for their comprehensive shop directory.\n\nIf you’re tired of brick-and-mortar shops, hit up a weekend market for fresh local farm produce and savour the taste of Western Australia. You may also wish to pick up some handmade gifts for your friends back home.\n\n### Where To Catch Up On Event Listings and News in Perth\n\n![community newspapers perth](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/newspaper.jpg)\n\n![community newspapers perth](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/newspaper.jpg)\n\n_Image: Pixabay_\n\nGrab a ticket to an event in Perth this weekend, or catch a local live performance during your travel dates with tips from Visit Perth City and Experience Perth. These websites come updated with regular event listings of exhibitions, markets, film screenings, performances, and other festivals and community activities.\n\n[PerthNow](http://www.perthnow.com.au/), [The West Australian](https://thewest.com.au/latest), and [WA Today](http://www.watoday.com.au/) are also indispensable go-to sources of Perth updates before your trip. Beyond these, you could also do a quick scroll through [Google News’ Perth page](https://www.google.com.sg/search?q=google+news+perth&rlz=1C1CHBF_enSG696SG696&oq=google+news+perth&aqs=chrome..69i57j69i64.1930j0j7&sourceid=chrome&ie=UTF-8#tbm=nws&q=perth) to get you up to speed on local events on the go.\n\nNo time to do your own research? Simply drop by Pan Pacific Perth’s concierge desk to seek the expert opinions of our [Les Clefs d’Or Concierges](http://blog.panpacific.com/hotels/meet-our-stellar-concierges-pan-pacific-perth/).\n\n**Opening hours:**\n\nPerth city shopping hours: Monday to Thursday: 9:00am to 9:00pm, Friday: 9:00am to 9:00pm, Saturday: 9:00am to 5:00pm, Sunday: noon to 6:00pm.\n\nFremantle shopping hours: Monday to Thursday: 8:00am to 7:00pm, Friday: 8:00am to 9:00pm, Saturday: 8:00am to 5:00pm, Sunday: 11:00am to 6:00pm.\n\n### Where To Stay In Perth For Business Travel\n\n![perth hotels cbd 5 star](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/pan-pacific-perth-premier-room.jpg)\n\n![perth hotels cbd 5 star](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/welcome-to-perth-preparing-for-your-trip/pan-pacific-perth-premier-room.jpg)\n\n_Image: Pan Pacific_\n\nLast but certainly not least, you will need a comfortable place to stay. Business travellers can take advantage of the central location of our 486-room Pan Pacific Perth, situated on the Swan River in the main business district.\n\nTake a close look at the LED interactive televisions, iPhone docking stations, high-speed Wi-Fi, and new fittings and lighting in the bathrooms. Following a recent refurbishment exercise, the hotel’s Premier Rooms and suites now come with award-winning HASSELL interior designs.\n\nFeaturing the warm hues of the Pacific Rim, our newly refreshed lobby and business centre contains Mac computers, printing facilities, a conference table, and lounges for meetings. You can enjoy complimentary WiFi at our entire hotel and lobby areas, or chat our with our friendly and knowledgeable Lobby Ambassadors if you need help with travel recommendations.\n\n**Have a good stay at the City of Light. [Welcome to Perth!](https://www.panpacific.com/en/hotels-and-resorts/pp-perth.html)**\n\n### Sign Up for Pan Pacific DISCOVERY\n\nJoin Pan Pacific DISCOVERY and unlock a wealth of benefits, with or without a stay. Enjoy exclusive member rates, specially curated Local Offers and Experiences, and access to our rewards currency, DISCOVERY Dollars (D$).\n\n[SIGN UP NOW](https://www.panpacific.com/en/panpacific-discovery/sign-up-today.html)\n\n## More to Discover\n\n- [Australia](http://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/all-articles.html?country=australia)\n- [Perth](http://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/all-articles.html?city=perth)\n- [Business Traveller](http://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/all-articles.html?persona=business-traveller)\n- [City Walk](http://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/all-articles.html?interest=city-walk)\n- [First-Time Visitors](http://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/all-articles.html?interest=first-time-visitors)\n\n- [![Sydney, Melbourne or Perth: Which City to Travel to?](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/destination-insights/australia/all-cities/sydney-melbourne-or-perth-which-city-to-travel-to/T24-20-australia-where-to-travel-parkroyal-picks.jpg)](https://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/australia/australia-articles/sydney-melbourne-or-perth.html)\n[Australia](https://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/australia.html) [**Sydney, Melbourne or Perth: Which City to Travel to?**](https://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/australia/australia-articles/sydney-melbourne-or-perth.html)\n\n  - [Perth](http://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/all-articles.html?city=perth) [City Walk](http://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/all-articles.html?interest=city-walk)\n\n- [![Things To Do In Perth: Discover These Secret Spots](https://www.panpacific.com/content/dam/pphg-revamp/en/ppper/the-hotel/destination-insights/signpost/cottesloe-beach-secret-spots-perth-signpost.jpg)](https://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/australia/perth/things-to-do-in-perth.html)\n[Australia](https://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/australia.html) [**Things To Do In Perth: Discover These Secret Spots**](https://www.panpacific.com/en/insights/australia/perth/things-to-do-in-perth.html)\n\n  -         "url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth",
        "title": "Transperth - Wikipedia",
        "description": "[Jump to content](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#bodyContent)\n\nFrom Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia\n\nSuburban public transport system serving Perth, Western Australia\n\nThis article is about the public transport system in Perth, Western Australia. For the organisation known as Transperth from 1986 to 1995, see [Metropolitan Transport Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Transport_Trust).\n\n\"Public transport in Perth\" redirects here. For public transport in Perth, Scotland, see [Public transport in Perth and Kinross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport_in_Perth_and_Kinross).\n\n**Transperth** is the public transport system for [Perth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth) and surrounding areas in [Western Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Australia). It is managed by the [Public Transport Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Transport_Authority_(Western_Australia)) (PTA), a state government organisation, and consists of train, bus and ferry services. Bus operations are contracted out to [Swan Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swan_Transit), [Path Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Path_Transit) and [Transdev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transdev_WA). Ferry operations are contracted out to [Captain Cook Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captain_Cook_Cruises_Western_Australia). Train operations are done by the PTA through their Transperth Train Operations division.\n\nThe Transperth train system consists of eight lines: the [Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport_line,_Perth), [Armadale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armadale_line), [Ellenbrook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellenbrook_line), [Fremantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fremantle_line), [Mandurah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandurah_line), [Midland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midland_line,_Perth), [Thornlie–Cockburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thornlie%E2%80%93Cockburn_line), and [Yanchep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanchep_line) lines; and 85 stations. Train services have operated since 1881. The Transperth bus system has over 1,499 buses and 51 bus stations, 38 of which are interchanges at railway stations. Buses have operated under private operators from 1903; these private operators were bought out by the [Metropolitan Transport Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Transport_Trust) starting in 1958. Contracting out the bus operations began in the mid-1990s. The Transperth ferry system has one route, two stops, and three ferries. The system extends as far north as [Two Rocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Rocks) on the outer north of the [Perth metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_metropolitan_area), [Wundowie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wundowie,_Western_Australia) in the [Shire of Northam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shire_of_Northam), and [Waroona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waroona,_Western_Australia) in the [Shire of Waroona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shire_of_Waroona). Fares are paid using the [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider) system or using cash or contactless payments. With 123,307,689 boardings in the year to June 2023, Transperth has the fourth highest patronage in Australia, although the train system has Australia's third highest train patronage.\n\n## Ticketing\n[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Transperth&action=edit&section=6)]\n\n[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Challis_Station%2C_Kelmscott%2C_Western_Australia%2C_August_2022_25.jpg/250px-Challis_Station%2C_Kelmscott%2C_Western_Australia%2C_August_2022_25.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Challis_Station,_Kelmscott,_Western_Australia,_August_2022_25.jpg) Information, emergency, and ticket machines at [Challis station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Challis_station)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Transperth_SmartRider_Processor_Perth_Train_Station.jpg/250px-Transperth_SmartRider_Processor_Perth_Train_Station.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Transperth_SmartRider_Processor_Perth_Train_Station.jpg) [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider) tag machine and [fare gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fare_gate) behind at [Perth station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_station)\n\nThe fare structure and ticketing system is the same for the bus, train and ferry network. A number of ticket types are available for purchase by travellers, which can be paid for using [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider) cards, cash or contactless payments.\n\nThe Transperth system is split into nine concentric zones, radiating out from the centre of Perth. [[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_2021%E2%80%932022-14):26[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Zone_Map-11) Zone one extends 8 kilometres (5.0mi) from the centre of Perth, zones two and three consist of bands 9 kilometres (5.6mi) wide, and the remaining zones consist of bands 10 kilometres (6.2mi) wide.\n\nPrior to January 2022, travelling within one zone cost a one-zone fare, with higher fares applied to travellers using two or more zones, up to a nine zone fare for travelling though all nine zones. Multiple trips, including transfers between modes and services, were possible under the same fare as long as this is done within two hours for journeys four zones or less or three hours for journeys of five zones or more. [[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_2021%E2%80%932022-14):26[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Fares-15) In January 2022, a two-zone fare cap was instituted by the McGowan government as an election promise prior to the [2021 state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Western_Australian_state_election). [[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-16)[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-17) Since February 2024, travel has been free for everyone with a [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider) each Sunday. [[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-18) In January 2026, the two-zone fare cap was replaced by a flat \"Go Anywhere\" fare for unlimited travel within three hours across the entire Transperth network. [[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-19)\n\nThere are other ticket types available for passengers. The \"two-section\" fare is less than a one-zone fare and is for one-way trips below 3.2 kilometres (2mi). Primary and secondary school students with a Student SmartRider can travel for free on weekdays during the school year, and with a concession fare other days. [[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-20) The DayRider ticket allows unlimited travel for one day. The FamilyRider ticket allows unlimited travel for up to seven people for one day under certain conditions and at certain times. [[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-21)\n\nAs of 2022, 79% of all fares were paid for using a [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider) card,[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_2021%E2%80%932022-14):27 a contactless [stored-value card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stored-value_card) introduced in 2007. Paying for paper tickets using cash is also possible. Tickets can be issued on all buses and ferries and from [ticket machines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ticket_machines) at train stations and ferry jetties. [[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_2021%E2%80%932022-14):26 SmartRiders can only be purchased at certain retail stores, Transperth InfoCentres, and at SmartRider Hub machines at selected bus and railway stations; they are not purchasable at every station or on buses. Methods for adding money to the SmartRider card include using SmartRider Hub and stand alone \"add-value\" machines at certain stations, on buses and ferries, at certain retail stores and Transperth InfoCentres, [BPAY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BPAY) through online banking, and by setting up \"Autoload\", where direct debits are made automatically from a bank account to a SmartRider. Fares for using a SmartRider cost 10% less than paper tickets, and 20% less if \"Autoload\" is used. [[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_2021%E2%80%932022-14):27 To use a SmartRider card, users must \"tag on\" at the start of a journey and \"tag off\" at the end of their journey, and the lowest possible fare is automatically applied. The daily spend on a SmartRider is capped at the DayRider cash fare.\n\nPeople travelling to and from events at Perth Stadium do not have to use a SmartRider or pay for a ticket as Transperth fares are included in the cost of every Perth Stadium ticket. [[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-22)\n\nFrom the 1990s to 2007, [MultiRiders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MultiRider) were used as multi-trip tickets. They used magnetic-stripe technology. [[21]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-23)\n\nFrom December 2025, commuters became able to use contactless payment methods such as credit and debit cards and digital wallets on smartphones and smartwatches to tag on and pay at the start of a journey. Commuters using this method will pay the cash fare rate. [[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-24)\n\n### Free Transit Zone\n[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Transperth&action=edit&section=7)]\n\nPerth has a Free Transit Zone (FTZ) with zero-fare travel on buses and trains in its central business district.\n\nOn the rail network, however, free travel within the zone is only available to passengers who have a [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider) card or use contactless payment, and tag on and off. This was not always the case, but is now required due to changes in station structures brought about by the implementation of the SmartRider ticketing system. [[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-25) For train passengers, the zone is bounded by [City West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_West_railway_station), [Elizabeth Quay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Quay_railway_station), and [Claisebrook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claisebrook_railway_station) stations. [[24]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-26)\n\nThe FTZ is funded by the [Perth Parking Levy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_Parking_Levy), a levy on non-residential parking bays in Perth, East Perth, West Perth and Northbridge. [[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-27) The levy was introduced under the Perth Parking Management Act 1999, and allows levy funds to be used for transport-related investments aimed at reducing private car travel into and out of Perth CBD, including the FTZ and CAT bus services. [[26]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-28)\n\n## References\n1.         "markdown": "[Jump to content](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#bodyContent)\n\nFrom Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia\n\nSuburban public transport system serving Perth, Western Australia\n\nThis article is about the public transport system in Perth, Western Australia. For the organisation known as Transperth from 1986 to 1995, see [Metropolitan Transport Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Transport_Trust \"Metropolitan Transport Trust\").\n\n\"Public transport in Perth\" redirects here. For public transport in Perth, Scotland, see [Public transport in Perth and Kinross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport_in_Perth_and_Kinross \"Public transport in Perth and Kinross\").\n\n| Transperth |\n| --- |\n| (train)[\\[a\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Number_of_train_stations-1)<br>54 (bus)[\\[b\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-2) |\n| Annual ridership | 161,223,754 (year to June 2026) |\n| Headquarters | [Public Transport Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Transport_Centre \"Public Transport Centre\") |\n| Website | (former)<br>[MTT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Transport_Trust \"Metropolitan Transport Trust\") (former) |\n| Number of vehicles | 1690 buses<br>342 railcars<br>3 ferries |\n| Technical |\n| [Track gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Track_gauge \"Track gauge\") | [1,067mm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3_ft_6_in_gauge_railways \"3 ft 6 in gauge railways\") (3ft6in)<br>[narrow gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrow-gauge_railway \"Narrow-gauge railway\") |\n| [Electrification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway_electrification \"Railway electrification\") | [25kV50HzAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25_kV_AC_railway_electrification \"25 kV AC railway electrification\") from [overhead catenary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overhead_line \"Overhead line\") |\n\n**Transperth** is the public transport system for [Perth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth \"Perth\") and surrounding areas in [Western Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Australia \"Western Australia\"). It is managed by the [Public Transport Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Transport_Authority_(Western_Australia) \"Public Transport Authority (Western Australia)\") (PTA), a state government organisation, and consists of train, bus and ferry services. Bus operations are contracted out to [Swan Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swan_Transit \"Swan Transit\"), [Path Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Path_Transit \"Path Transit\") and [Transdev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transdev_WA \"Transdev WA\"). Ferry operations are contracted out to [Captain Cook Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captain_Cook_Cruises_Western_Australia \"Captain Cook Cruises Western Australia\"). Train operations are done by the PTA through their Transperth Train Operations division.\n\nThe Transperth train system consists of eight lines: the [Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport_line,_Perth \"Airport line, Perth\"), [Armadale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armadale_line \"Armadale line\"), [Ellenbrook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellenbrook_line \"Ellenbrook line\"), [Fremantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fremantle_line \"Fremantle line\"), [Mandurah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandurah_line \"Mandurah line\"), [Midland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midland_line,_Perth \"Midland line, Perth\"), [Thornlie–Cockburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thornlie%E2%80%93Cockburn_line \"Thornlie–Cockburn line\"), and [Yanchep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanchep_line \"Yanchep line\") lines; and 85 stations. Train services have operated since 1881. The Transperth bus system has over 1,499 buses and 51 bus stations, 38 of which are interchanges at railway stations. Buses have operated under private operators from 1903; these private operators were bought out by the [Metropolitan Transport Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Transport_Trust \"Metropolitan Transport Trust\") starting in 1958. Contracting out the bus operations began in the mid-1990s. The Transperth ferry system has one route, two stops, and three ferries. The system extends as far north as [Two Rocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Rocks \"Two Rocks\") on the outer north of the [Perth metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_metropolitan_area \"Perth metropolitan area\"), [Wundowie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wundowie,_Western_Australia \"Wundowie, Western Australia\") in the [Shire of Northam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shire_of_Northam \"Shire of Northam\"), and [Waroona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waroona,_Western_Australia \"Waroona, Western Australia\") in the [Shire of Waroona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shire_of_Waroona \"Shire of Waroona\"). Fares are paid using the [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider \"SmartRider\") system or using cash or contactless payments. With 123,307,689 boardings in the year to June 2023, Transperth has the fourth highest patronage in Australia, although the train system has Australia's third highest train patronage.\n\n## History\n\n\\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Transperth&action=edit&section=1 \"Edit section: History\")\\]\n\nThe first railway in [Perth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth \"Perth\") was the [Eastern Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Railway_(Western_Australia) \"Eastern Railway (Western Australia)\") from [Fremantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fremantle \"Fremantle\") to [Guildford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guildford,_Western_Australia \"Guildford, Western Australia\") via Perth, which opened on 1 March 1881. [Western Australian Government Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Australian_Government_Railways \"Western Australian Government Railways\") (WAGR) was formed in 1890 as the operator of railways in Perth and [Western Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Australia \"Western Australia\"). The [South Western Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Western_Railway,_Western_Australia \"South Western Railway, Western Australia\") opened on 2 May 1893. On 28 September 1899, the first [trams in Perth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trams_in_Perth \"Trams in Perth\") began operating.[\\[1\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-PTA_history-3)\n\nIn 1903, the first [private bus operator began](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buses_in_Perth \"Buses in Perth\"), operating routes from [Victoria Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Park,_Western_Australia \"Victoria Park, Western Australia\") to the Perth central business district (CBD). [Trams in Fremantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trams_in_Fremantle \"Trams in Fremantle\") began operating in 1905 and were abolished in 1952. The first diesel railcars, the [WAGR ADG class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WAGR_ADG_class \"WAGR ADG class\"), entered service on 28 November 1954, replacing steam locomotives. This allowed for shorter stop spacings, leading to the opening of seven new railway stations on that date.[\\[1\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-PTA_history-3)\n\nOn 15 January 1958, the [Metropolitan Transport Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Transport_Trust \"Metropolitan Transport Trust\") (MTT) was formed, and began to acquire the various private bus operators in Perth. The MTT also operated Perth's ferry services. Perth's last tram ran in 1958 as well. By 5 October 1968, suburban rail services were fully operated by diesel railcars. In the late-1960s, the [Midland line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midland_line,_Perth \"Midland line, Perth\") was converted from narrow gauge to dual gauge, allowing standard gauge trains to travel from the eastern states to [East Perth railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Perth_railway_station \"East Perth railway station\"). The gauge conversion necessitated the rebuild of several stations and bridges along the Midland line. In 1969, the last [trolleybuses in Perth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trolleybuses_in_Perth \"Trolleybuses in Perth\") ran. The management of Perth's public transport was integrated into a single body in 1974 when the MTT took over the management of Perth's suburban rail services from WAGR. The MTT contracted out the operations of the suburban rail services back to WAGR.[\\[1\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-PTA_history-3) Throughout the 1970s, bus stations opened across the Perth metropolitan region, starting with Morley bus station (now [Galleria bus station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galleria_bus_station \"Galleria bus station\")) in August 1972 and [Wellington Street bus station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington_Street_bus_station \"Wellington Street bus station\") in March 1973.[\\[2\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_1998-4)\n\nThe [Fremantle line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fremantle_line \"Fremantle line\") was closed to passenger services on 1 September 1979, despite a 100,000 signature petition calling for the closure to be cancelled. Following a change in government at the [1983 state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Western_Australian_state_election \"1983 Western Australian state election\"), the Fremantle line was reopened. The government then decided to electrify the existing suburban rail lines and build the [Yanchep line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanchep_line \"Yanchep line\") (known at the time as the Joondalup line). On 31 August 1986, the MTT adopted Transperth as its [trading name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trading_name \"Trading name\"), marking the first time that the name _Transperth_ has been used for Perth's public transport system. Construction for the Joondalup line commenced on 14 November 1989. The electrification of the rail network was completed by September 1991 and the [Transperth A-series trains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth_A-series_train \"Transperth A-series train\") entered service, replacing the diesel railcars. The Joondalup line partially opened on 21 December 1992 and fully opened in March 1993.[\\[1\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-PTA_history-3)[\\[2\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_1998-4)[\\[3\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Newman-5) Later that year, the Joondalup line was extended to [Currambine railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Currambine_railway_station \"Currambine railway station\").[\\[4\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-6)[\\[5\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-7)\n\nThe operation of Transperth services underwent a reorganisation in the mid-1990s, with bus and ferry operations contracted out to private operators. Responsibility for managing the system was transferred to the Department of Transport. The Transperth brand was also transferred to the Department of Transport, with the brand to be used by all the companies which were to operate services under contract. The MTT adopted the trading name _MetroBus_ on 14 February 1995. The operation of the ferries was transferred to [Captain Cook Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captain_Cook_Cruises_Western_Australia \"Captain Cook Cruises Western Australia\") in February 1995. It was initially planned that MetroBus would compete with the private sector for contracts, and it was successful in bidding for the Joondalup North, Armadale South and [Perth CATs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_Central_Area_Transit \"Perth Central Area Transit\") contracts in 1995 and 1996 whilst several other contracts were awarded to the private sector. The government later announced that it would withdraw MetroBus from operating and it would tender out the remaining contract areas. MetroBus ceased operating bus services on 4 July 1998.[\\[2\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_1998-4)[\\[3\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Newman-5)\n\nIn December 1999, enabling legislation for the [Mandurah line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandurah_line \"Mandurah line\") was passed, paving the way for the construction of the line. It was initially planned to branch off the [Armadale line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armadale_line \"Armadale line\"), but was later rerouted to go via a direct route south of Perth in the median of the [Kwinana Freeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwinana_Freeway \"Kwinana Freeway\"), necessitating tunnelling under the Perth CBD. This led to the development of the [Thornlie–Cockburn line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thornlie%E2%80%93Cockburn_line \"Thornlie–Cockburn line\") as a branch off the Armadale line instead. On 1 July 2003, the [Public Transport Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Transport_Authority_(Western_Australia) \"Public Transport Authority (Western Australia)\") was formed, taking over from the Department of Transport of the ownership and management of Transperth services. An extension of the Joondalup line to [Clarkson station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarkson_railway_station,_Perth \"Clarkson railway station, Perth\") opened on 4 October 2004. The first [Transperth B-series trains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth_B-series_train \"Transperth B-series train\") enter service in 2004 as well and [Nowergup railcar depot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowergup_railcar_depot \"Nowergup railcar depot\") opens. [Thornlie station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thornlie_railway_station \"Thornlie railway station\") opened on 7 August 2005, and on 23 December 2007, the Mandurah line opened.[\\[1\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-PTA_history-3)[\\[3\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Newman-5)\n\nOn 21 September 2014, an extension of the Joondalup line to [Butler station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butler_railway_station \"Butler railway station\") opened. In July 2016, the [Perth Busport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_Busport \"Perth Busport\") opened, replacing Wellington Street bus station. The busport is underground and has a dynamic bus stand allocation system to increase capacity for the same number of bus stands. On 3 November 2016, construction begins on the [Forrestfield–Airport Link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forrestfield%E2%80%93Airport_Link \"Forrestfield–Airport Link\"), later to become known as the [Airport line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport_line,_Perth \"Airport line, Perth\"). In 2018 and 2019, the government passed legislation to construct an extension of the Joondalup line to [Yanchep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanchep_railway_station \"Yanchep railway station\"), the [Ellenbrook line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellenbrook_line \"Ellenbrook line\") as a spur off the Midland line, and an extension of the Thornlie line to [Cockburn Central station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cockburn_Central_railway_station \"Cockburn Central railway station\") along the Mandurah line, completing the original plans for the Mandurah line to spur off the Armadale line. These projects are all part of the [Metronet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metronet_(Western_Australia) \"Metronet (Western Australia)\") project to improve Perth's public transport system, which involves several station rebuilds and improvements as well. The Airport line opened on 9 October 2022 and the Yanchep extension opened on 14 July 2024, coinciding with the renaming of the Joondalup line to the Yanchep line. In January 2023, testing for the [Transperth C-series train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth_C-series_train \"Transperth C-series train\") commenced.[\\[1\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-PTA_history-3) These trains entered service on 8 April 2024.[\\[6\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-The_West_Australian_19_March_2024-8)[\\[7\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Media_Statements_19_March_2024-9) The [Ellenbrook line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellenbrook_line \"Ellenbrook line\") opened on 8 December 2024, while 8 June 2025 saw the opening of the [Thornlie–Cockburn line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thornlie%E2%80%93Cockburn_line \"Thornlie–Cockburn line\"). An extension to [Byford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byford_railway_station \"Byford railway station\") of the Armadale line opened on 12 October 2025.\n\n## Services\n\n\\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Transperth&action=edit&section=2 \"Edit section: Services\")\\]\n\nTransperth services run as far as [Two Rocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Rocks \"Two Rocks\") on the outer north of the [Perth metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_metropolitan_area \"Perth metropolitan area\"), [Wundowie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wundowie,_Western_Australia \"Wundowie, Western Australia\") in the [Shire of Northam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shire_of_Northam \"Shire of Northam\"), and [Bouvard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bouvard,_Western_Australia \"Bouvard, Western Australia\") in the south of [Mandurah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandurah \"Mandurah\").[\\[8\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-10)\n\n### Train\n\n\\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Transperth&action=edit&section=3 \"Edit section: Train\")\\]\n\nMain article: [Railways in Perth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railways_in_Perth \"Railways in Perth\")\n\nSee also: [List of Transperth railway stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Transperth_railway_stations \"List of Transperth railway stations\")\n\n[![Map of the Transperth rail network](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/TransperthRailwayMap.svg/250px-TransperthRailwayMap.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TransperthRailwayMap.svg) Transperth rail map during early 2025\n\nThe Transperth train system consists of eight lines: the [Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport_line,_Perth \"Airport line, Perth\"), [Armadale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armadale_line \"Armadale line\"), [Ellenbrook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellenbrook_line \"Ellenbrook line\"), [Fremantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fremantle_line \"Fremantle line\"), [Mandurah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandurah_line \"Mandurah line\"), [Midland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midland_line,_Perth \"Midland line, Perth\"), [Thornlie–Cockburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thornlie%E2%80%93Cockburn_line \"Thornlie–Cockburn line\"), and [Yanchep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanchep_line \"Yanchep line\") lines. These lines all meet at [Perth station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_railway_station \"Perth railway station\") and [Perth Underground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_Underground_railway_station \"Perth Underground railway station\") (used by the Yanchep and Mandurah lines) in the city's CBD, radiating out from there. The [Thornlie-Cockburn Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thornlie%E2%80%93Cockburn_line \"Thornlie–Cockburn line\") has been recently built and was opened in mid 2025. There are several other extensions and upgrades under construction as well under the Metronet project. The system has 85 stations along these eight lines.[\\[a\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Number_of_train_stations-1)[\\[9\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Zone_Map-11) Transperth is Australia's third busiest passenger rail system, behind [Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Trains \"Sydney Trains\") and [Melbourne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Trains_Melbourne \"Metro Trains Melbourne\") but ahead of [Brisbane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queensland_Rail_Citytrain_network \"Queensland Rail Citytrain network\") and [Adelaide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railways_in_Adelaide \"Railways in Adelaide\").[\\[10\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-BITRE-12) There are three trains used: the [Transperth A-series train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth_A-series_train \"Transperth A-series train\"), [Transperth B-series train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth_B-series_train \"Transperth B-series train\") and the [Transperth C-series train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth_C-series_train \"Transperth C-series train\") which entered service on 8 April 2024.[\\[6\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-The_West_Australian_19_March_2024-8)[\\[7\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Media_Statements_19_March_2024-9)\n\n### Bus\n\n\\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Transperth&action=edit&section=4 \"Edit section: Bus\")\\]\n\nMain article: [Buses in Perth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buses_in_Perth \"Buses in Perth\")\n\nSee also: [List of Transperth bus stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Transperth_bus_stations \"List of Transperth bus stations\") and [List of bus routes in Perth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bus_routes_in_Perth \"List of bus routes in Perth\")\n\n[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Transperth_Bus_Number_2762.jpg/250px-Transperth_Bus_Number_2762.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Transperth_Bus_Number_2762.jpg) A bus operated by Path Transit\n\nThe Transperth bus system consists of 44 bus-train interchanges and 13 bus-only stations.[\\[9\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Zone_Map-11) The bus system is contracted out to private operators: [Swan Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swan_Transit \"Swan Transit\"), [Path Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Path_Transit \"Path Transit\") and [Transdev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transdev_WA \"Transdev WA\").[\\[11\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-13) The bus system is the fourth-busiest in Australia, behind [Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buses_in_Sydney \"Buses in Sydney\"), [Melbourne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buses_in_Melbourne \"Buses in Melbourne\") and [Brisbane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Translink_(Queensland) \"Translink (Queensland)\").[\\[10\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-BITRE-12)\n\n### Ferry\n\n\\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Transperth&action=edit&section=5 \"Edit section: Ferry\")\\]\n\nThe Transperth ferry system consists of one route and three ferries. The line across the [Swan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swan_River_(Western_Australia) \"Swan River (Western Australia)\") from [Elizabeth Quay Jetty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Quay_Jetty \"Elizabeth Quay Jetty\") in the Perth CBD to [Mends Street Jetty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mends_Street_Jetty \"Mends Street Jetty\") in [South Perth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Perth,_Western_Australia \"South Perth, Western Australia\"). The ferries are the [MV _Shelley Taylor-Smith_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MV_Shelley_Taylor-Smith \"MV Shelley Taylor-Smith\"), the [MV _Phillip Pendal_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MV_Phillip_Pendal \"MV Phillip Pendal\") and the [MV _Tricia_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MV_Tricia \"MV Tricia\").\n\nIn 2024, prior to the Western Australian State Election, the Cook-Labour government announced their plans to expand the ferry service to Applecross and Crawley (UWA). The project is estimated to cost $107 million and include.\n\n- Two new jetties for ferries in Applecross and Crawley near UWA.\n- Improvements to Mends Street jetty in South Perth.\n- 5 New locally made electric ferries to replace and expand the current fleet.\n\n## Ticketing\n\n\\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Transperth&action=edit&section=6 \"Edit section: Ticketing\")\\]\n\n[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Challis_Station%2C_Kelmscott%2C_Western_Australia%2C_August_2022_25.jpg/250px-Challis_Station%2C_Kelmscott%2C_Western_Australia%2C_August_2022_25.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Challis_Station,_Kelmscott,_Western_Australia,_August_2022_25.jpg) Information, emergency, and ticket machines at [Challis station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Challis_station \"Challis station\")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Transperth_SmartRider_Processor_Perth_Train_Station.jpg/250px-Transperth_SmartRider_Processor_Perth_Train_Station.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Transperth_SmartRider_Processor_Perth_Train_Station.jpg) [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider \"SmartRider\") tag machine and [fare gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fare_gate \"Fare gate\") behind at [Perth station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_station \"Perth station\")\n\nThe fare structure and ticketing system is the same for the bus, train and ferry network. A number of ticket types are available for purchase by travellers, which can be paid for using [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider \"SmartRider\") cards, cash or contactless payments.\n\nThe Transperth system is split into nine concentric zones, radiating out from the centre of Perth.[\\[12\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_2021%E2%80%932022-14):26[\\[9\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Zone_Map-11) Zone one extends 8 kilometres (5.0mi) from the centre of Perth, zones two and three consist of bands 9 kilometres (5.6mi) wide, and the remaining zones consist of bands 10 kilometres (6.2mi) wide.\n\nPrior to January 2022, travelling within one zone cost a one-zone fare, with higher fares applied to travellers using two or more zones, up to a nine zone fare for travelling though all nine zones. Multiple trips, including transfers between modes and services, were possible under the same fare as long as this is done within two hours for journeys four zones or less or three hours for journeys of five zones or more.[\\[12\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_2021%E2%80%932022-14):26[\\[13\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Fares-15) In January 2022, a two-zone fare cap was instituted by the McGowan government as an election promise prior to the [2021 state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Western_Australian_state_election \"2021 Western Australian state election\").[\\[14\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-16)[\\[15\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-17) Since February 2024, travel has been free for everyone with a [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider \"SmartRider\") each Sunday.[\\[16\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-18) In January 2026, the two-zone fare cap was replaced by a flat \"Go Anywhere\" fare for unlimited travel within three hours across the entire Transperth network.[\\[17\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-19)\n\nThere are other ticket types available for passengers. The \"two-section\" fare is less than a one-zone fare and is for one-way trips below 3.2 kilometres (2mi). Primary and secondary school students with a Student SmartRider can travel for free on weekdays during the school year, and with a concession fare other days.[\\[18\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-20) The DayRider ticket allows unlimited travel for one day. The FamilyRider ticket allows unlimited travel for up to seven people for one day under certain conditions and at certain times.[\\[19\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-21)\n\nAs of 2022, 79% of all fares were paid for using a [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider \"SmartRider\") card,[\\[12\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_2021%E2%80%932022-14):27 a contactless [stored-value card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stored-value_card \"Stored-value card\") introduced in 2007. Paying for paper tickets using cash is also possible. Tickets can be issued on all buses and ferries and from [ticket machines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ticket_machines \"Ticket machines\") at train stations and ferry jetties.[\\[12\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_2021%E2%80%932022-14):26 SmartRiders can only be purchased at certain retail stores, Transperth InfoCentres, and at SmartRider Hub machines at selected bus and railway stations; they are not purchasable at every station or on buses. Methods for adding money to the SmartRider card include using SmartRider Hub and stand alone \"add-value\" machines at certain stations, on buses and ferries, at certain retail stores and Transperth InfoCentres, [BPAY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BPAY \"BPAY\") through online banking, and by setting up \"Autoload\", where direct debits are made automatically from a bank account to a SmartRider. Fares for using a SmartRider cost 10% less than paper tickets, and 20% less if \"Autoload\" is used.[\\[12\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Annual_Report_2021%E2%80%932022-14):27 To use a SmartRider card, users must \"tag on\" at the start of a journey and \"tag off\" at the end of their journey, and the lowest possible fare is automatically applied. The daily spend on a SmartRider is capped at the DayRider cash fare.\n\nPeople travelling to and from events at Perth Stadium do not have to use a SmartRider or pay for a ticket as Transperth fares are included in the cost of every Perth Stadium ticket.[\\[20\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-22)\n\nFrom the 1990s to 2007, [MultiRiders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MultiRider \"MultiRider\") were used as multi-trip tickets. They used magnetic-stripe technology.[\\[21\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-23)\n\nFrom December 2025, commuters became able to use contactless payment methods such as credit and debit cards and digital wallets on smartphones and smartwatches to tag on and pay at the start of a journey. Commuters using this method will pay the cash fare rate.[\\[22\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-24)\n\n| Ticket Type | Standard | Concession |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| 2 Section | $2.40 | $1.10 |\n| Go Anywhere | $3.50 | $1.60 |\n| DayRider | $7.00 | $3.20 |\n| FamilyRider | $7.00 | N/A |\n\nCash and contactless payment fares\n\nas of 1 January 2026[\\[13\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-Fares-15)\n\n### Free Transit Zone\n\n\\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Transperth&action=edit&section=7 \"Edit section: Free Transit Zone\")\\]\n\nPerth has a Free Transit Zone (FTZ) with zero-fare travel on buses and trains in its central business district.\n\nOn the rail network, however, free travel within the zone is only available to passengers who have a [SmartRider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SmartRider \"SmartRider\") card or use contactless payment, and tag on and off. This was not always the case, but is now required due to changes in station structures brought about by the implementation of the SmartRider ticketing system.[\\[23\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-25) For train passengers, the zone is bounded by [City West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_West_railway_station \"City West railway station\"), [Elizabeth Quay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Quay_railway_station \"Elizabeth Quay railway station\"), and [Claisebrook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claisebrook_railway_station \"Claisebrook railway station\") stations.[\\[24\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-26)\n\nThe FTZ is funded by the [Perth Parking Levy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_Parking_Levy \"Perth Parking Levy\"), a levy on non-residential parking bays in Perth, East Perth, West Perth and Northbridge.[\\[25\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-27) The levy was introduced under the Perth Parking Management Act 1999, and allows levy funds to be used for transport-related investments aimed at reducing private car travel into and out of Perth CBD, including the FTZ and CAT bus services.[\\[26\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-28)\n\n### Parking fees\n\n\\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Transperth&action=edit&section=8 \"Edit section: Parking fees\")\\]\n\nPaid car parking at railway stations was introduced on 1 July 2014. The cost is $2 per weekday, free on weekends. SmartRiders or cash can be used to pay for parking.[\\[27\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-29)[\\[28\\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transperth#cite_note-30)\n\n##
